Yeah, uh… I came in because things at home have just been getting to me more than they usually do. Ever since my father-in-law moved in with us, I can still keep it together on the ambulance and at work—kind of have to, you know—but when I get home, I’m more wound up, more short with people than I like.

And I’m taking some coursework too, and that’s been harder to keep up with. I used to blow off steam with fishing, woodworking, even these first-aid workshops I’d go to—funny, since I do that for a living, but I like teaching people basics—and lately I’ve just been skipping all that. Yeah, I guess I have. Not avoiding work—I still go in and do what needs doing, because you can’t exactly half-show-up as an EMT. But once I’m home, or when I’m supposed to be doing coursework, I get wound up and short, and then I start dropping the things I normally make time for.

Since my father-in-law moved in, I just keep skipping them. I tell myself I’m too tired or there’s stuff to deal with at home, and then I end up not going.

Detached from myself… I’m not totally sure what that would feel like. I mean, there are times after a long day where everything feels kind of automatic, like you drive home and realize you don’t remember every turn—but I think everybody does that, right?

Repetitive thoughts or behaviors… like checking the stove ten times, or needing things lined up a certain way? No, I don’t think I’ve had anything like that.

I tell myself I’m too tired or there’s stuff to deal with at home, and then I end up not going.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're detached from yourself or the world around you?\n\nPatient: Detached from myself… I’m not totally sure what that would feel like. Yeah… mostly the stuff at home that starts to feel like another demand, if that makes sense. Since my father-in-law moved in with us, I feel like I’m holding it together fine enough at work, because I have to, but when I get home I’m already wound up and I get short quicker than I want to.

But once I’m home, or when I’m supposed to be doing coursework, I get wound up and short, and then I start dropping the things I normally make time for.\n\nLike fishing, woodworking, those first-aid workshops… which sounds funny because the workshops are sort of work-adjacent, but they usually help me feel useful and clear-headed. Since my father-in-law moved in, I just keep skipping them. I tell myself I’m too tired or there’s stuff to deal with at home, and then I end up not going.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're detached from yourself or the world around you?\n\nPatient: Detached from myself… I’m not totally sure what that would feel like. I mean, there are times after a long day where everything feels kind of automatic, like you drive home and realize you don’t remember every turn—but I think everybody does that, right?\n\nMy wife used to joke that I could find my way to the bait shop half-asleep, which, honestly, was probably true. But as far as feeling like I’m outside my body or the world isn’t real… no, I don’t think I’ve really had that.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having any repetitive thoughts or behaviors that you feel compelled to do?\n\nPatient: Repetitive thoughts or behaviors… like checking the stove ten times, or needing things lined up a certain way? No, I don’t think I’ve had anything like that.\n\nI mean, I can get stuck thinking through things sometimes, especially if there’s a lot going on, but I don’t feel like I have to do some ritual or I can’t move on. Although my wife would probably say I have a “system” for the garage, but that’s just because if you don’t keep tools where they belong you spend half the weekend looking for a socket wrench. So, no—not compelled like that, I don’t think.\n\nDoctor: Are there specific things or situations at home that you're trying to avoid when you feel wound up?\n\nPatient: Yeah… mostly the stuff at home that starts to feel like another demand, if that makes sense. Since my father-in-law moved in with us, I feel like I’m holding it together fine enough at work, because I have to, but when I get home I’m already wound up and I get short quicker than I want to.\n\nAnd then coursework is the big one I’ll avoid—I’ll sit down to do it and suddenly I’m reorganizing something or telling myself I’ll do it later. Same with the things that usually help me reset, like fishing or woodworking. Even the first-aid workshops, which I used to like going to… I’ve been skipping those too. I tell myself there’s too much going on at home, but I think I’m just trying not to deal with feeling so wound up.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m not sure I’d put it that way. I don’t think I’m avoiding places because I’m afraid something bad is going to happen there.

It’s more like… if I know a situation is going to be tense or one more thing to handle, I just don’t want to be in it. But not like, “If I go there, something terrible will happen.” It’s not that specific. I mean, I’ve been in plenty of stressful places with my job, and that’s different somehow—you just do what needs doing.

At home, though, or with certain plans, I’ll kind of steer away from them because I don’t have the bandwidth. But fear of the place itself? No, I haven’t really noticed that.

I’ve even been skipping the stuff that usually clears my head, like fishing, woodworking, those first-aid workshops. Four months or so.\n\nDoctor: Are you avoiding certain situations or places because you fear something might happen, or because you're anxious about being in those situations?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it that way. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t know… I wouldn’t say I walk around thinking it’ll never get better, exactly. It’s more like when I’m in the middle of it, it feels bigger than it probably is.

Like, I’ll be standing in the kitchen and there’s just noise and people moving around and I start thinking, “How is this supposed to work long-term?” But then, you know, I’ve seen situations change. In my job, you learn that a scene can look completely unmanageable and then five minutes later it’s different because somebody makes one decision or clears one thing out of the way.

So… I guess I don’t feel hopeless, if that’s what you mean. Just worn down sometimes.

I used to blow off steam with fishing, woodworking, even these first-aid workshops I’d go to—funny, since I do that for a living, but I like teaching people basics—and lately I’ve just been skipping all that. I guess I’m here because that’s not really like me.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling anxious lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah… anxious, or maybe wound up is the word I’d use. Since my father-in-law moved in with us, I can keep myself steady at work—on calls you just do what needs doing—but when I get home I’m shorter than I mean to be. \n\nAnd with my coursework, I’ll sit down and try to focus, and then I’m just… keyed up. I’ve even been skipping the stuff that usually clears my head, like fishing, woodworking, those first-aid workshops. Which is odd for me, because normally that’s how I reset.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or down lately?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it sad, exactly. I mean, everybody has days where they’re kind of low, right? Like after a rough shift or when the weather’s lousy and you realize you still haven’t fixed the garage shelf you said you’d fix two months ago.\n\nBut… sad or down as a main thing? I haven’t really noticed that, no. It’s more that things feel off, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any episodes where you suddenly felt extremely frightened or overwhelmed for no apparent reason?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it that way. I mean, I’ve been overwhelmed plenty of times—comes with the job sometimes, and honestly family stuff can pile up in a different way—but suddenly extremely frightened out of nowhere? I don’t think so.\n\nLike, I know what it feels like when adrenaline kicks in on a bad call, but that has a reason. This is more… I don’t know, general strain, I guess. Not like some sudden attack of fear for no reason.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding certain situations or activities because of feeling overwhelmed or wound up?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I guess I have. Not avoiding work—I still go in and do what needs doing, because you can’t exactly half-show-up as an EMT. But once I’m home, or when I’m supposed to be doing coursework, I get wound up and short, and then I start dropping the things I normally make time for.\n\nLike fishing, woodworking, those first-aid workshops… which sounds funny because the workshops are sort of work-adjacent, but they usually help me feel useful and clear-headed. Since my father-in-law moved in, I just keep skipping them. I mean, there are times after a long day where everything feels kind of automatic, like you drive home and realize you don’t remember every turn—but I think everybody does that, right?\n\nMy wife used to joke that I could find my way to the bait shop half-asleep, which, honestly, was probably true. But as far as feeling like I’m outside my body or the world isn’t real… no, I don’t think I’ve really had that.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having any repetitive thoughts or behaviors that you feel compelled to do?\n\nPatient: Repetitive thoughts or behaviors… like checking the stove ten times, or needing things lined up a certain way? ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I guess I have been avoiding some things, though I don’t think of it as “anxiety” right away.

Like, I used to go fishing, and I’ve got this little woodworking setup—nothing fancy, just enough to make a mess and maybe a crooked shelf if I’m lucky. And I’d sometimes help with first-aid workshops too, which is funny because I do that kind of thing for work, but teaching it is different. It clears my head.

Lately I’ve been skipping that stuff. Since my father-in-law moved in, I can hold it together at work pretty well, but when I get home, or when I’m trying to deal with coursework, I’m already wound up and shorter than I mean to be. So if something takes planning or extra energy, even something I normally like, I just… don’t go.

I used to blow off steam with fishing, woodworking, even these first-aid workshops I’d go to—funny, since I do that for a living, but I like teaching people basics—and lately I’ve just been skipping all that. Since my father-in-law moved in with us, I can keep myself steady at work—on calls you just do what needs doing—but when I get home I’m shorter than I mean to be. \n\nAnd with my coursework, I’ll sit down and try to focus, and then I’m just… keyed up. I’ve even been skipping the stuff that usually clears my head, like fishing, woodworking, those first-aid workshops. Which is odd for me, because normally that’s how I reset.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or down lately?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it sad, exactly. I mean, everybody has days where they’re kind of low, right? I haven’t really noticed that, no. I mean, I’ve been overwhelmed plenty of times—comes with the job sometimes, and honestly family stuff can pile up in a different way—but suddenly extremely frightened out of nowhere? I don’t think so.\n\nLike, I know what it feels like when adrenaline kicks in on a bad call, but that has a reason. This is more… I don’t know, general strain, I guess. Not like some sudden attack of fear for no reason.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding certain situations or activities because of feeling overwhelmed or wound up?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I guess I have. Not avoiding work—I still go in and do what needs doing, because you can’t exactly half-show-up as an EMT. But once I’m home, or when I’m supposed to be doing coursework, I get wound up and short, and then I start dropping the things I normally make time for.\n\nLike fishing, woodworking, those first-aid workshops… which sounds funny because the workshops are sort of work-adjacent, but they usually help me feel useful and clear-headed. Since my father-in-law moved in, I just keep skipping them. I tell myself I’m too tired or there’s stuff to deal with at home, and then I end up not going.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're detached from yourself or the world around you?\n\nPatient: Detached from myself… I’m not totally sure what that would feel like. I mean, there are times after a long day where everything feels kind of automatic, like you drive home and realize you don’t remember every turn—but I think everybody does that, right?\n\nMy wife used to joke that I could find my way to the bait shop half-asleep, which, honestly, was probably true. But as far as feeling like I’m outside my body or the world isn’t real… no, I don’t think I’ve really had that.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having any repetitive thoughts or behaviors that you feel compelled to do?\n\nPatient: Repetitive thoughts or behaviors… like checking the stove ten times, or needing things lined up a certain way? I tell myself there’s too much going on at home, but I think I’m just trying not to deal with feeling so wound up.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way, with everything feeling off and you being more wound up than usual?\n\nPatient: About four months, I think. It’s more like when I’m in the middle of it, it feels bigger than it probably is.\n\nLike, I’ll be standing in the kitchen and there’s just noise and people moving around and I start thinking, “How is this supposed to work long-term?” But then, you know, I’ve seen situations change. In my job, you learn that a scene can look completely unmanageable and then five minutes later it’s different because somebody makes one decision or clears one thing out of the way.\n\nSo… I guess I don’t feel hopeless, if that’s what you mean. Just worn down sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Are there any specific activities or situations that you've been avoiding because they make you feel anxious?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I have been avoiding some things, though I don’t think of it as “anxiety” right away.\n\nLike, I used to go fishing, and I’ve got this little woodworking setup—nothing fancy, just enough to make a mess and maybe a crooked shelf if I’m lucky. And I’d sometimes help with first-aid workshops too, which is funny because I do that kind of thing for work, but teaching it is different. It clears my head.\n\nLately I’ve been skipping that stuff. Since my father-in-law moved in, I can hold it together at work pretty well, but when I get home, or when I’m trying to deal with coursework, I’m already wound up and shorter than I mean to be. So if something takes planning or extra energy, even something I normally like, I just… don’t go.\n\n[Last Question]\nAre there any places or social situations that you're avoiding because they make you feel anxious or uncomfortable?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`. Do NOT force an unrelated item.\n- On your first reply of the consultation, always return the item whose key is your\n  chief_complaint_symptom_code, whatever the doctor opened with.\n- Do not answer the doctor, and do not write anything outside the JSON.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"key\": \"<key>\", \"name\": \"<name>\", \"description\": \"<description>\", \"reason\": \"<why this item>\"}\n"
  },

I don’t think I’m avoiding places because I’m afraid something bad is going to happen there.\n\nIt’s more like… if I know a situation is going to be tense or one more thing to handle, I just don’t want to be in it. It’s more like when I’m in the middle of it, it feels bigger than it probably is.\n\nLike, I’ll be standing in the kitchen and there’s just noise and people moving around and I start thinking, “How is this supposed to work long-term?” But then, you know, I’ve seen situations change. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says.     "role": "user",

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I guess it’s less a particular place and more the situations that come with it, if that makes sense.

Like, I came in because since my father-in-law moved in with us, I can still keep it together at work—somehow when the radio goes off and there’s a call, you just switch into that mode. But then at home, or when I’m trying to sit down with coursework, I’m already kind of wound tight. I get short faster than I used to.

So I’ve been skipping things I normally would go do—fishing, messing around with woodworking, even helping with first-aid workshops. Those usually clear my head, but lately they feel like one more thing to manage. Not because I’m scared of the people there exactly… more like I’m uncomfortable and tired before I even get there.

    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n13\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF43.0, F43.1, F43.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Yeah, uh… I came in because things at home have just been getting to me more than they usually do. Ever since my father-in-law moved in with us, I can still keep it together on the ambulance and at work—kind of have to, you know—but when I get home, I’m more wound up, more short with people than I like.\n\nAnd I’m taking some coursework too, and that’s been harder to keep up with. I used to blow off steam with fishing, woodworking, even these first-aid workshops I’d go to—funny, since I do that for a living, but I like teaching people basics—and lately I’ve just been skipping all that. I guess I’m here because that’s not really like me.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling anxious lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah… anxious, or maybe wound up is the word I’d use. Since my father-in-law moved in with us, I can keep myself steady at work—on calls you just do what needs doing—but when I get home I’m shorter than I mean to be. \n\nAnd with my coursework, I’ll sit down and try to focus, and then I’m just… keyed up. I’ve even been skipping the stuff that usually clears my head, like fishing, woodworking, those first-aid workshops. Which is odd for me, because normally that’s how I reset.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or down lately?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it sad, exactly. I mean, everybody has days where they’re kind of low, right? Like after a rough shift or when the weather’s lousy and you realize you still haven’t fixed the garage shelf you said you’d fix two months ago.\n\nBut… sad or down as a main thing? I haven’t really noticed that, no. It’s more that things feel off, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any episodes where you suddenly felt extremely frightened or overwhelmed for no apparent reason?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it that way. I mean, I’ve been overwhelmed plenty of times—comes with the job sometimes, and honestly family stuff can pile up in a different way—but suddenly extremely frightened out of nowhere? I don’t think so.\n\nLike, I know what it feels like when adrenaline kicks in on a bad call, but that has a reason. This is more… I don’t know, general strain, I guess. Not like some sudden attack of fear for no reason.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding certain situations or activities because of feeling overwhelmed or wound up?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I guess I have. Not avoiding work—I still go in and do what needs doing, because you can’t exactly half-show-up as an EMT. But once I’m home, or when I’m supposed to be doing coursework, I get wound up and short, and then I start dropping the things I normally make time for.\n\nLike fishing, woodworking, those first-aid workshops… which sounds funny because the workshops are sort of work-adjacent, but they usually help me feel useful and clear-headed. Since my father-in-law moved in, I just keep skipping them. I tell myself I’m too tired or there’s stuff to deal with at home, and then I end up not going.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're detached from yourself or the world around you?\n\nPatient: Detached from myself… I’m not totally sure what that would feel like. I mean, there are times after a long day where everything feels kind of automatic, like you drive home and realize you don’t remember every turn—but I think everybody does that, right?\n\nMy wife used to joke that I could find my way to the bait shop half-asleep, which, honestly, was probably true. But as far as feeling like I’m outside my body or the world isn’t real… no, I don’t think I’ve really had that.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having any repetitive thoughts or behaviors that you feel compelled to do?\n\nPatient: Repetitive thoughts or behaviors… like checking the stove ten times, or needing things lined up a certain way? No, I don’t think I’ve had anything like that.\n\nI mean, I can get stuck thinking through things sometimes, especially if there’s a lot going on, but I don’t feel like I have to do some ritual or I can’t move on. Although my wife would probably say I have a “system” for the garage, but that’s just because if you don’t keep tools where they belong you spend half the weekend looking for a socket wrench. So, no—not compelled like that, I don’t think.\n\nDoctor: Are there specific things or situations at home that you're trying to avoid when you feel wound up?\n\nPatient: Yeah… mostly the stuff at home that starts to feel like another demand, if that makes sense. Since my father-in-law moved in with us, I feel like I’m holding it together fine enough at work, because I have to, but when I get home I’m already wound up and I get short quicker than I want to.\n\nAnd then coursework is the big one I’ll avoid—I’ll sit down to do it and suddenly I’m reorganizing something or telling myself I’ll do it later. Same with the things that usually help me reset, like fishing or woodworking. Even the first-aid workshops, which I used to like going to… I’ve been skipping those too. I tell myself there’s too much going on at home, but I think I’m just trying not to deal with feeling so wound up.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way, with everything feeling off and you being more wound up than usual?\n\nPatient: About four months, I think. It kind of lines up with when my father-in-law moved into our house.\n\nAt first I figured it was just the usual adjustment period, you know—everybody getting used to where the coffee mugs go and who leaves lights on and all that. My house has always had its own rhythm, and then suddenly it didn’t. But yeah… if I’m being honest, that’s when I started feeling more wound up than usual. Four months or so.\n\nDoctor: Are you avoiding certain situations or places because you fear something might happen, or because you're anxious about being in those situations?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it that way. I don’t think I’m avoiding places because I’m afraid something bad is going to happen there.\n\nIt’s more like… if I know a situation is going to be tense or one more thing to handle, I just don’t want to be in it. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I think so. Mostly at home. I mean, I came in because ever since my father-in-law moved in with us, I can still hold it together at work—work is work, you know, you’ve got a job in front of you and you do it.

But at home I’m more wound up. Shorter with people than I’m used to being. And then with coursework, too, it’s like I sit down and I’m already irritated before anything even happens.

With friends, I guess I’ve pulled back some. I’ve been skipping fishing, not doing much woodworking, and I haven’t been helping with the first-aid workshops like I used to. Those things usually keep my head clear, but lately they just feel like one more thing I have to fit in. So… yeah, I’d say it’s affected things, even if I haven’t really wanted to admit that.

    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n14\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF43.0, F43.1, F43.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Yeah, uh… I came in because things at home have just been getting to me more than they usually do. Ever since my father-in-law moved in with us, I can still keep it together on the ambulance and at work—kind of have to, you know—but when I get home, I’m more wound up, more short with people than I like.\n\nAnd I’m taking some coursework too, and that’s been harder to keep up with. I used to blow off steam with fishing, woodworking, even these first-aid workshops I’d go to—funny, since I do that for a living, but I like teaching people basics—and lately I’ve just been skipping all that. I guess I’m here because that’s not really like me.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling anxious lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah… anxious, or maybe wound up is the word I’d use. Since my father-in-law moved in with us, I can keep myself steady at work—on calls you just do what needs doing—but when I get home I’m shorter than I mean to be. \n\nAnd with my coursework, I’ll sit down and try to focus, and then I’m just… keyed up. I’ve even been skipping the stuff that usually clears my head, like fishing, woodworking, those first-aid workshops. Which is odd for me, because normally that’s how I reset.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or down lately?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it sad, exactly. I mean, everybody has days where they’re kind of low, right? Like after a rough shift or when the weather’s lousy and you realize you still haven’t fixed the garage shelf you said you’d fix two months ago.\n\nBut… sad or down as a main thing? I haven’t really noticed that, no. It’s more that things feel off, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any episodes where you suddenly felt extremely frightened or overwhelmed for no apparent reason?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it that way. I mean, I’ve been overwhelmed plenty of times—comes with the job sometimes, and honestly family stuff can pile up in a different way—but suddenly extremely frightened out of nowhere? I don’t think so.\n\nLike, I know what it feels like when adrenaline kicks in on a bad call, but that has a reason. This is more… I don’t know, general strain, I guess. Not like some sudden attack of fear for no reason.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding certain situations or activities because of feeling overwhelmed or wound up?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I guess I have. Not avoiding work—I still go in and do what needs doing, because you can’t exactly half-show-up as an EMT. But once I’m home, or when I’m supposed to be doing coursework, I get wound up and short, and then I start dropping the things I normally make time for.\n\nLike fishing, woodworking, those first-aid workshops… which sounds funny because the workshops are sort of work-adjacent, but they usually help me feel useful and clear-headed. Since my father-in-law moved in, I just keep skipping them. I tell myself I’m too tired or there’s stuff to deal with at home, and then I end up not going.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're detached from yourself or the world around you?\n\nPatient: Detached from myself… I’m not totally sure what that would feel like. I mean, there are times after a long day where everything feels kind of automatic, like you drive home and realize you don’t remember every turn—but I think everybody does that, right?\n\nMy wife used to joke that I could find my way to the bait shop half-asleep, which, honestly, was probably true. But as far as feeling like I’m outside my body or the world isn’t real… no, I don’t think I’ve really had that.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having any repetitive thoughts or behaviors that you feel compelled to do?\n\nPatient: Repetitive thoughts or behaviors… like checking the stove ten times, or needing things lined up a certain way? No, I don’t think I’ve had anything like that.\n\nI mean, I can get stuck thinking through things sometimes, especially if there’s a lot going on, but I don’t feel like I have to do some ritual or I can’t move on. Although my wife would probably say I have a “system” for the garage, but that’s just because if you don’t keep tools where they belong you spend half the weekend looking for a socket wrench. So, no—not compelled like that, I don’t think.\n\nDoctor: Are there specific things or situations at home that you're trying to avoid when you feel wound up?\n\nPatient: Yeah… mostly the stuff at home that starts to feel like another demand, if that makes sense. Since my father-in-law moved in with us, I feel like I’m holding it together fine enough at work, because I have to, but when I get home I’m already wound up and I get short quicker than I want to.\n\nAnd then coursework is the big one I’ll avoid—I’ll sit down to do it and suddenly I’m reorganizing something or telling myself I’ll do it later. Same with the things that usually help me reset, like fishing or woodworking. Even the first-aid workshops, which I used to like going to… I’ve been skipping those too. I tell myself there’s too much going on at home, but I think I’m just trying not to deal with feeling so wound up.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way, with everything feeling off and you being more wound up than usual?\n\nPatient: About four months, I think. It kind of lines up with when my father-in-law moved into our house.\n\nAt first I figured it was just the usual adjustment period, you know—everybody getting used to where the coffee mugs go and who leaves lights on and all that. My house has always had its own rhythm, and then suddenly it didn’t. But yeah… if I’m being honest, that’s when I started feeling more wound up than usual. Four months or so.\n\nDoctor: Are you avoiding certain situations or places because you fear something might happen, or because you're anxious about being in those situations?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it that way. I don’t think I’m avoiding places because I’m afraid something bad is going to happen there.\n\nIt’s more like… if I know a situation is going to be tense or one more thing to handle, I just don’t want to be in it. But not like, “If I go there, something terrible will happen.” It’s not that specific. I mean, I’ve been in plenty of stressful places with my job, and that’s different somehow—you just do what needs doing.\n\nAt home, though, or with certain plans, I’ll kind of steer away from them because I don’t have the bandwidth. But fear of the place itself? No, I haven’t really noticed that.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like things will never get better?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I wouldn’t say I walk around thinking it’ll never get better, exactly. It’s more like when I’m in the middle of it, it feels bigger than it probably is.\n\nLike, I’ll be standing in the kitchen and there’s just noise and people moving around and I start thinking, “How is this supposed to work long-term?” But then, you know, I’ve seen situations change. In my job, you learn that a scene can look completely unmanageable and then five minutes later it’s different because somebody makes one decision or clears one thing out of the way.\n\nSo… I guess I don’t feel hopeless, if that’s what you mean. Just worn down sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Are there any specific activities or situations that you've been avoiding because they make you feel anxious?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I have been avoiding some things, though I don’t think of it as “anxiety” right away.\n\nLike, I used to go fishing, and I’ve got this little woodworking setup—nothing fancy, just enough to make a mess and maybe a crooked shelf if I’m lucky. And I’d sometimes help with first-aid workshops too, which is funny because I do that kind of thing for work, but teaching it is different. It clears my head.\n\nLately I’ve been skipping that stuff. Since my father-in-law moved in, I can hold it together at work pretty well, but when I get home, or when I’m trying to deal with coursework, I’m already wound up and shorter than I mean to be. So if something takes planning or extra energy, even something I normally like, I just… don’t go.\n\nDoctor: Are there any places or social situations that you're avoiding because they make you feel anxious or uncomfortable?\n\nPatient: I guess it’s less a particular place and more the situations that come with it, if that makes sense.\n\nLike, I came in because since my father-in-law moved in with us, I can still keep it together at work—somehow when the radio goes off and there’s a call, you just switch into that mode. But then at home, or when I’m trying to sit down with coursework, I’m already kind of wound tight. I get short faster than I used to.\n\nSo I’ve been skipping things I normally would go do—fishing, messing around with woodworking, even helping with first-aid workshops. I’ve been skipping fishing, not doing much woodworking, and I haven’t been helping with the first-aid workshops like I used to. Those things usually keep my head clear, but lately they just feel like one more thing I have to fit in. My house has always had its own rhythm, and then suddenly it didn’t. But yeah… if I’m being honest, that’s when I started feeling more wound up than usual. Four months or so.\n\nDoctor: Are you avoiding certain situations or places because you fear something might happen, or because you're anxious about being in those situations?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it that way. I don’t think I’m avoiding places because I’m afraid something bad is going to happen there.\n\nIt’s more like… if I know a situation is going to be tense or one more thing to handle, I just don’t want to be in it. But not like, “If I go there, something terrible will happen.” It’s not that specific. I mean, I’ve been in plenty of stressful places with my job, and that’s different somehow—you just do what needs doing.\n\nAt home, though, or with certain plans, I’ll kind of steer away from them because I don’t have the bandwidth. No, I haven’t really noticed that.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like things will never get better?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I wouldn’t say I walk around thinking it’ll never get better, exactly. It’s more like when I’m in the middle of it, it feels bigger than it probably is.\n\nLike, I’ll be standing in the kitchen and there’s just noise and people moving around and I start thinking, “How is this supposed to work long-term?” But then, you know, I’ve seen situations change. I get short faster than I used to.\n\nSo I’ve been skipping things I normally would go do—fishing, messing around with woodworking, even helping with first-aid workshops. I don’t think so… not like I’m afraid I’m going to fall apart in public or anything.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Yeah, uh… I came in because things at home have just been getting to me more than they usually do. I used to blow off steam with fishing, woodworking, even these first-aid workshops I’d go to—funny, since I do that for a living, but I like teaching people basics—and lately I’ve just been skipping all that. Since my father-in-law moved in with us, I can keep myself steady at work—on calls you just do what needs doing—but when I get home I’m shorter than I mean to be. \n\nAnd with my coursework, I’ll sit down and try to focus, and then I’m just… keyed up. I’ve even been skipping the stuff that usually clears my head, like fishing, woodworking, those first-aid workshops. Not like some sudden attack of fear for no reason.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding certain situations or activities because of feeling overwhelmed or wound up?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I guess I have. Not avoiding work—I still go in and do what needs doing, because you can’t exactly half-show-up as an EMT. But once I’m home, or when I’m supposed to be doing coursework, I get wound up and short, and then I start dropping the things I normally make time for.\n\nLike fishing, woodworking, those first-aid workshops… which sounds funny because the workshops are sort of work-adjacent, but they usually help me feel useful and clear-headed. Since my father-in-law moved in, I just keep skipping them. I tell myself I’m too tired or there’s stuff to deal with at home, and then I end up not going.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're detached from yourself or the world around you?\n\nPatient: Detached from myself… I’m not totally sure what that would feel like. I mean, there are times after a long day where everything feels kind of automatic, like you drive home and realize you don’t remember every turn—but I think everybody does that, right?\n\nMy wife used to joke that I could find my way to the bait shop half-asleep, which, honestly, was probably true. But as far as feeling like I’m outside my body or the world isn’t real… no, I don’t think I’ve really had that.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having any repetitive thoughts or behaviors that you feel compelled to do?\n\nPatient: Repetitive thoughts or behaviors… like checking the stove ten times, or needing things lined up a certain way? No, I don’t think I’ve had anything like that.\n\nI mean, I can get stuck thinking through things sometimes, especially if there’s a lot going on, but I don’t feel like I have to do some ritual or I can’t move on. Although my wife would probably say I have a “system” for the garage, but that’s just because if you don’t keep tools where they belong you spend half the weekend looking for a socket wrench. So, no—not compelled like that, I don’t think.\n\nDoctor: Are there specific things or situations at home that you're trying to avoid when you feel wound up?\n\nPatient: Yeah… mostly the stuff at home that starts to feel like another demand, if that makes sense. Since my father-in-law moved in with us, I feel like I’m holding it together fine enough at work, because I have to, but when I get home I’m already wound up and I get short quicker than I want to.\n\nAnd then coursework is the big one I’ll avoid—I’ll sit down to do it and suddenly I’m reorganizing something or telling myself I’ll do it later. Same with the things that usually help me reset, like fishing or woodworking. Even the first-aid workshops, which I used to like going to… I’ve been skipping those too. I tell myself there’s too much going on at home, but I think I’m just trying not to deal with feeling so wound up.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way, with everything feeling off and you being more wound up than usual?\n\nPatient: About four months, I think. It kind of lines up with when my father-in-law moved into our house.\n\nAt first I figured it was just the usual adjustment period, you know—everybody getting used to where the coffee mugs go and who leaves lights on and all that. My house has always had its own rhythm, and then suddenly it didn’t. But yeah… if I’m being honest, that’s when I started feeling more wound up than usual. Four months or so.\n\nDoctor: Are you avoiding certain situations or places because you fear something might happen, or because you're anxious about being in those situations?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it that way. I don’t think I’m avoiding places because I’m afraid something bad is going to happen there.\n\nIt’s more like… if I know a situation is going to be tense or one more thing to handle, I just don’t want to be in it. But not like, “If I go there, something terrible will happen.” It’s not that specific. I mean, I’ve been in plenty of stressful places with my job, and that’s different somehow—you just do what needs doing.\n\nAt home, though, or with certain plans, I’ll kind of steer away from them because I don’t have the bandwidth. But fear of the place itself? No, I haven’t really noticed that.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like things will never get better?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I wouldn’t say I walk around thinking it’ll never get better, exactly. It’s more like when I’m in the middle of it, it feels bigger than it probably is.\n\nLike, I’ll be standing in the kitchen and there’s just noise and people moving around and I start thinking, “How is this supposed to work long-term?” But then, you know, I’ve seen situations change. In my job, you learn that a scene can look completely unmanageable and then five minutes later it’s different because somebody makes one decision or clears one thing out of the way.\n\nSo… I guess I don’t feel hopeless, if that’s what you mean. Just worn down sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Are there any specific activities or situations that you've been avoiding because they make you feel anxious?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I have been avoiding some things, though I don’t think of it as “anxiety” right away.\n\nLike, I used to go fishing, and I’ve got this little woodworking setup—nothing fancy, just enough to make a mess and maybe a crooked shelf if I’m lucky. And I’d sometimes help with first-aid workshops too, which is funny because I do that kind of thing for work, but teaching it is different. It clears my head.\n\nLately I’ve been skipping that stuff. Since my father-in-law moved in, I can hold it together at work pretty well, but when I get home, or when I’m trying to deal with coursework, I’m already wound up and shorter than I mean to be. So if something takes planning or extra energy, even something I normally like, I just… don’t go.\n\nDoctor: Are there any places or social situations that you're avoiding because they make you feel anxious or uncomfortable?\n\nPatient: I guess it’s less a particular place and more the situations that come with it, if that makes sense.\n\nLike, I came in because since my father-in-law moved in with us, I can still keep it together at work—somehow when the radio goes off and there’s a call, you just switch into that mode. But then at home, or when I’m trying to sit down with coursework, I’m already kind of wound tight. I get short faster than I used to.\n\nSo I’ve been skipping things I normally would go do—fishing, messing around with woodworking, even helping with first-aid workshops. Those usually clear my head, but lately they feel like one more thing to manage. Not because I’m scared of the people there exactly… more like I’m uncomfortable and tired before I even get there.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your relationships with family or friends since you started feeling this way?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. Mostly at home. I mean, I came in because ever since my father-in-law moved in with us, I can still hold it together at work—work is work, you know, you’ve got a job in front of you and you do it.\n\nBut at home I’m more wound up. Shorter with people than I’m used to being. And then with coursework, too, it’s like I sit down and I’m already irritated before anything even happens.\n\nWith friends, I guess I’ve pulled back some. I’ve been skipping fishing, not doing much woodworking, and I haven’t been helping with the first-aid workshops like I used to. Those things usually keep my head clear, but lately they just feel like one more thing I have to fit in. So… yeah, I’d say it’s affected things, even if I haven’t really wanted to admit that.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding situations because you're afraid of having some kind of emotional breakdown or losing control?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not like I’m afraid I’m going to fall apart in public or anything.\n\nI mean, losing control is kind of a loaded phrase in my line of work—when you’re on a scene, you’re always thinking about keeping things calm, keeping your voice steady, even when somebody’s yelling or there’s too many people standing around. Funny thing is, I can usually do that fine.\n\nSo, no, I don’t think I’m avoiding things because I’m scared of some big emotional breakdown. It’s more… I just don’t feel like I’ve got the extra bandwidth, if that makes sense.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Yeah… it’s been about four months now, I’d say. Pretty much since my father-in-law moved into our house.

At first I thought it was just the normal adjustment period, you know—like when anyone moves in and everyone’s routine gets knocked around. We had to shift things in the house, make space, figure out meals and noise and all that little stuff people don’t think about until it’s happening. It kind of lines up with when my father-in-law moved into our house.\n\nAt first I figured it was just the usual adjustment period, you know—everybody getting used to where the coffee mugs go and who leaves lights on and all that. My house has always had its own rhythm, and then suddenly it didn’t. But yeah… if I’m being honest, that’s when I started feeling more wound up than usual. Four months or so.\n\nDoctor: Are you avoiding certain situations or places because you fear something might happen, or because you're anxious about being in those situations?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it that way. I don’t think I’m avoiding places because I’m afraid something bad is going to happen there.\n\nIt’s more like… if I know a situation is going to be tense or one more thing to handle, I just don’t want to be in it. But not like, “If I go there, something terrible will happen.” It’s not that specific. I mean, I’ve been in plenty of stressful places with my job, and that’s different somehow—you just do what needs doing.\n\nAt home, though, or with certain plans, I’ll kind of steer away from them because I don’t have the bandwidth. But fear of the place itself? No, I haven’t really noticed that.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like things will never get better?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I wouldn’t say I walk around thinking it’ll never get better, exactly. It’s more like when I’m in the middle of it, it feels bigger than it probably is.\n\nLike, I’ll be standing in the kitchen and there’s just noise and people moving around and I start thinking, “How is this supposed to work long-term?” But then, you know, I’ve seen situations change. In my job, you learn that a scene can look completely unmanageable and then five minutes later it’s different because somebody makes one decision or clears one thing out of the way.\n\nSo… I guess I don’t feel hopeless, if that’s what you mean. Just worn down sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Are there any specific activities or situations that you've been avoiding because they make you feel anxious?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I have been avoiding some things, though I don’t think of it as “anxiety” right away.\n\nLike, I used to go fishing, and I’ve got this little woodworking setup—nothing fancy, just enough to make a mess and maybe a crooked shelf if I’m lucky. And I’d sometimes help with first-aid workshops too, which is funny because I do that kind of thing for work, but teaching it is different. It clears my head.\n\nLately I’ve been skipping that stuff. Since my father-in-law moved in, I can hold it together at work pretty well, but when I get home, or when I’m trying to deal with coursework, I’m already wound up and shorter than I mean to be. So if something takes planning or extra energy, even something I normally like, I just… don’t go.\n\nDoctor: Are there any places or social situations that you're avoiding because they make you feel anxious or uncomfortable?\n\nPatient: I guess it’s less a particular place and more the situations that come with it, if that makes sense.\n\nLike, I came in because since my father-in-law moved in with us, I can still keep it together at work—somehow when the radio goes off and there’s a call, you just switch into that mode. But then at home, or when I’m trying to sit down with coursework, I’m already kind of wound tight. I get short faster than I used to.\n\nSo I’ve been skipping things I normally would go do—fishing, messing around with woodworking, even helping with first-aid workshops. Those usually clear my head, but lately they feel like one more thing to manage. Not because I’m scared of the people there exactly… more like I’m uncomfortable and tired before I even get there.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your relationships with family or friends since you started feeling this way?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. Mostly at home. I mean, I came in because ever since my father-in-law moved in with us, I can still hold it together at work—work is work, you know, you’ve got a job in front of you and you do it.\n\nBut at home I’m more wound up. Shorter with people than I’m used to being. And then with coursework, too, it’s like I sit down and I’m already irritated before anything even happens.\n\nWith friends, I guess I’ve pulled back some. I’ve been skipping fishing, not doing much woodworking, and I haven’t been helping with the first-aid workshops like I used to. Those things usually keep my head clear, but lately they just feel like one more thing I have to fit in. So… yeah, I’d say it’s affected things, even if I haven’t really wanted to admit that.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding situations because you're afraid of having some kind of emotional breakdown or losing control?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not like I’m afraid I’m going to fall apart in public or anything.\n\nI mean, losing control is kind of a loaded phrase in my line of work—when you’re on a scene, you’re always thinking about keeping things calm, keeping your voice steady, even when somebody’s yelling or there’s too many people standing around. Funny thing is, I can usually do that fine.\n\nSo, no, I don’t think I’m avoiding things because I’m scared of some big emotional breakdown. It’s more… I just don’t feel like I’ve got the extra bandwidth, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Have your feelings of being wound up and anxious changed in intensity over the past four months?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it’s been about four months now, I’d say. Pretty much since my father-in-law moved into our house.\n\nAt first I thought it was just the normal adjustment period, you know—like when anyone moves in and everyone’s routine gets knocked around. We had to shift things in the house, make space, figure out meals and noise and all that little stuff people don’t think about until it’s happening. But it didn’t really just settle back down like I expected. So the wound-up feeling has kind of been there since then.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."

  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… some, but not across the board. Work-wise, like on the ambulance, I’m still doing my job. When there’s a call, I can focus because you kind of have to—there’s a rhythm to it, and everybody knows their role.

It’s more the stuff outside that. I’ve fallen behind on my paramedic coursework, which bugs me because I’m usually pretty steady with that kind of thing. I’ll sit down to do it and then I’m already tense, and it just… doesn’t happen.

And I haven’t been showing up as consistently for the community first-aid workshops I usually help with. I used to like doing those—teaching people CPR, bandaging, all that. Sometimes you get folks who are nervous and you can actually see them relax once they realize they can handle it. Lately, though, I keep putting it off or not making it there like I normally would. So at home and with those responsibilities, yeah, it’s gotten in the way some.

  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n17\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF43.0, F43.1, F43.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Yeah, uh… I came in because things at home have just been getting to me more than they usually do. Ever since my father-in-law moved in with us, I can still keep it together on the ambulance and at work—kind of have to, you know—but when I get home, I’m more wound up, more short with people than I like.\n\nAnd I’m taking some coursework too, and that’s been harder to keep up with. I used to blow off steam with fishing, woodworking, even these first-aid workshops I’d go to—funny, since I do that for a living, but I like teaching people basics—and lately I’ve just been skipping all that. I guess I’m here because that’s not really like me.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling anxious lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah… anxious, or maybe wound up is the word I’d use. Since my father-in-law moved in with us, I can keep myself steady at work—on calls you just do what needs doing—but when I get home I’m shorter than I mean to be. \n\nAnd with my coursework, I’ll sit down and try to focus, and then I’m just… keyed up. I’ve even been skipping the stuff that usually clears my head, like fishing, woodworking, those first-aid workshops. Which is odd for me, because normally that’s how I reset.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or down lately?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it sad, exactly. I mean, everybody has days where they’re kind of low, right? Like after a rough shift or when the weather’s lousy and you realize you still haven’t fixed the garage shelf you said you’d fix two months ago.\n\nBut… sad or down as a main thing? I haven’t really noticed that, no. It’s more that things feel off, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any episodes where you suddenly felt extremely frightened or overwhelmed for no apparent reason?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it that way. I mean, I’ve been overwhelmed plenty of times—comes with the job sometimes, and honestly family stuff can pile up in a different way—but suddenly extremely frightened out of nowhere? I don’t think so.\n\nLike, I know what it feels like when adrenaline kicks in on a bad call, but that has a reason. This is more… I don’t know, general strain, I guess. Not like some sudden attack of fear for no reason.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding certain situations or activities because of feeling overwhelmed or wound up?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I guess I have. Not avoiding work—I still go in and do what needs doing, because you can’t exactly half-show-up as an EMT. But once I’m home, or when I’m supposed to be doing coursework, I get wound up and short, and then I start dropping the things I normally make time for.\n\nLike fishing, woodworking, those first-aid workshops… which sounds funny because the workshops are sort of work-adjacent, but they usually help me feel useful and clear-headed. Since my father-in-law moved in, I just keep skipping them. I tell myself I’m too tired or there’s stuff to deal with at home, and then I end up not going.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're detached from yourself or the world around you?\n\nPatient: Detached from myself… I’m not totally sure what that would feel like. I mean, there are times after a long day where everything feels kind of automatic, like you drive home and realize you don’t remember every turn—but I think everybody does that, right?\n\nMy wife used to joke that I could find my way to the bait shop half-asleep, which, honestly, was probably true. But as far as feeling like I’m outside my body or the world isn’t real… no, I don’t think I’ve really had that.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having any repetitive thoughts or behaviors that you feel compelled to do?\n\nPatient: Repetitive thoughts or behaviors… like checking the stove ten times, or needing things lined up a certain way? No, I don’t think I’ve had anything like that.\n\nI mean, I can get stuck thinking through things sometimes, especially if there’s a lot going on, but I don’t feel like I have to do some ritual or I can’t move on. Although my wife would probably say I have a “system” for the garage, but that’s just because if you don’t keep tools where they belong you spend half the weekend looking for a socket wrench. So, no—not compelled like that, I don’t think.\n\nDoctor: Are there specific things or situations at home that you're trying to avoid when you feel wound up?\n\nPatient: Yeah… mostly the stuff at home that starts to feel like another demand, if that makes sense. Since my father-in-law moved in with us, I feel like I’m holding it together fine enough at work, because I have to, but when I get home I’m already wound up and I get short quicker than I want to.\n\nAnd then coursework is the big one I’ll avoid—I’ll sit down to do it and suddenly I’m reorganizing something or telling myself I’ll do it later. Same with the things that usually help me reset, like fishing or woodworking. Even the first-aid workshops, which I used to like going to… I’ve been skipping those too. I tell myself there’s too much going on at home, but I think I’m just trying not to deal with feeling so wound up.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way, with everything feeling off and you being more wound up than usual?\n\nPatient: About four months, I think. It kind of lines up with when my father-in-law moved into our house.\n\nAt first I figured it was just the usual adjustment period, you know—everybody getting used to where the coffee mugs go and who leaves lights on and all that. My house has always had its own rhythm, and then suddenly it didn’t. But yeah… if I’m being honest, that’s when I started feeling more wound up than usual. Four months or so.\n\nDoctor: Are you avoiding certain situations or places because you fear something might happen, or because you're anxious about being in those situations?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it that way. I don’t think I’m avoiding places because I’m afraid something bad is going to happen there.\n\nIt’s more like… if I know a situation is going to be tense or one more thing to handle, I just don’t want to be in it. But not like, “If I go there, something terrible will happen.” It’s not that specific. I mean, I’ve been in plenty of stressful places with my job, and that’s different somehow—you just do what needs doing.\n\nAt home, though, or with certain plans, I’ll kind of steer away from them because I don’t have the bandwidth. But fear of the place itself? No, I haven’t really noticed that.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like things will never get better?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I wouldn’t say I walk around thinking it’ll never get better, exactly. It’s more like when I’m in the middle of it, it feels bigger than it probably is.\n\nLike, I’ll be standing in the kitchen and there’s just noise and people moving around and I start thinking, “How is this supposed to work long-term?” But then, you know, I’ve seen situations change. In my job, you learn that a scene can look completely unmanageable and then five minutes later it’s different because somebody makes one decision or clears one thing out of the way.\n\nSo… I guess I don’t feel hopeless, if that’s what you mean. Just worn down sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Are there any specific activities or situations that you've been avoiding because they make you feel anxious?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I have been avoiding some things, though I don’t think of it as “anxiety” right away.\n\nLike, I used to go fishing, and I’ve got this little woodworking setup—nothing fancy, just enough to make a mess and maybe a crooked shelf if I’m lucky. And I’d sometimes help with first-aid workshops too, which is funny because I do that kind of thing for work, but teaching it is different. It clears my head.\n\nLately I’ve been skipping that stuff. Since my father-in-law moved in, I can hold it together at work pretty well, but when I get home, or when I’m trying to deal with coursework, I’m already wound up and shorter than I mean to be. So if something takes planning or extra energy, even something I normally like, I just… don’t go.\n\nDoctor: Are there any places or social situations that you're avoiding because they make you feel anxious or uncomfortable?\n\nPatient: I guess it’s less a particular place and more the situations that come with it, if that makes sense.\n\nLike, I came in because since my father-in-law moved in with us, I can still keep it together at work—somehow when the radio goes off and there’s a call, you just switch into that mode. But then at home, or when I’m trying to sit down with coursework, I’m already kind of wound tight. I get short faster than I used to.\n\nSo I’ve been skipping things I normally would go do—fishing, messing around with woodworking, even helping with first-aid workshops. Those usually clear my head, but lately they feel like one more thing to manage. Not because I’m scared of the people there exactly… more like I’m uncomfortable and tired before I even get there.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your relationships with family or friends since you started feeling this way?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. Mostly at home. I mean, I came in because ever since my father-in-law moved in with us, I can still hold it together at work—work is work, you know, you’ve got a job in front of you and you do it.\n\nBut at home I’m more wound up. Shorter with people than I’m used to being. And then with coursework, too, it’s like I sit down and I’m already irritated before anything even happens.\n\nWith friends, I guess I’ve pulled back some. I’ve been skipping fishing, not doing much woodworking, and I haven’t been helping with the first-aid workshops like I used to. Those things usually keep my head clear, but lately they just feel like one more thing I have to fit in. So… yeah, I’d say it’s affected things, even if I haven’t really wanted to admit that.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding situations because you're afraid of having some kind of emotional breakdown or losing control?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not like I’m afraid I’m going to fall apart in public or anything.\n\nI mean, losing control is kind of a loaded phrase in my line of work—when you’re on a scene, you’re always thinking about keeping things calm, keeping your voice steady, even when somebody’s yelling or there’s too many people standing around. Funny thing is, I can usually do that fine.\n\nSo, no, I don’t think I’m avoiding things because I’m scared of some big emotional breakdown. It’s more… I just don’t feel like I’ve got the extra bandwidth, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Have your feelings of being wound up and anxious changed in intensity over the past four months?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it’s been about four months now, I’d say. Pretty much since my father-in-law moved into our house.\n\nAt first I thought it was just the normal adjustment period, you know—like when anyone moves in and everyone’s routine gets knocked around. We had to shift things in the house, make space, figure out meals and noise and all that little stuff people don’t think about until it’s happening. But it didn’t really just settle back down like I expected. So the wound-up feeling has kind of been there since then.\n\nDoctor: Have you had difficulty performing your daily tasks or responsibilities at work or at home due to feeling wound up or anxious?\n\nPatient: Yeah… some, but not across the board. Work-wise, like on the ambulance, I’m still doing my job. I’ve fallen behind on my paramedic coursework, which bugs me because I’m usually pretty steady with that kind of thing. I’ll sit down to do it and then I’m already tense, and it just… doesn’t happen.\n\nAnd I haven’t been showing up as consistently for the community first-aid workshops I usually help with. I used to like doing those—teaching people CPR, bandaging, all that. Sometimes you get folks who are nervous and you can actually see them relax once they realize they can handle it. Lately, though, I keep putting it off or not making it there like I normally would. So at home and with those responsibilities, yeah, it’s gotten in the way some.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. But it didn’t really just settle back down like I expected. I’ve fallen behind on my paramedic coursework, which bugs me because I’m usually pretty steady with that kind of thing. Flashbacks? No, I don’t think so. Not like a specific scene that keeps replaying or anything like that.

I mean, in my job you see plenty of things you’d rather not carry around with you, and sometimes somebody will say something that reminds you of an old call—like even the smell of diesel and rain can put you right back near an accident scene for a second. But that’s not really what’s going on here, I don’t think.

I used to blow off steam with fishing, woodworking, even these first-aid workshops I’d go to—funny, since I do that for a living, but I like teaching people basics—and lately I’ve just been skipping all that. Since my father-in-law moved in with us, I can keep myself steady at work—on calls you just do what needs doing—but when I get home I’m shorter than I mean to be. \n\nAnd with my coursework, I’ll sit down and try to focus, and then I’m just… keyed up. I’ve even been skipping the stuff that usually clears my head, like fishing, woodworking, those first-aid workshops. Which is odd for me, because normally that’s how I reset.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or down lately?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it sad, exactly. I mean, everybody has days where they’re kind of low, right? Like after a rough shift or when the weather’s lousy and you realize you still haven’t fixed the garage shelf you said you’d fix two months ago.\n\nBut… sad or down as a main thing? I haven’t really noticed that, no. It’s more that things feel off, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any episodes where you suddenly felt extremely frightened or overwhelmed for no apparent reason?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it that way. I mean, I’ve been overwhelmed plenty of times—comes with the job sometimes, and honestly family stuff can pile up in a different way—but suddenly extremely frightened out of nowhere? I don’t think so.\n\nLike, I know what it feels like when adrenaline kicks in on a bad call, but that has a reason. This is more… I don’t know, general strain, I guess. Not like some sudden attack of fear for no reason.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding certain situations or activities because of feeling overwhelmed or wound up?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I guess I have. Not avoiding work—I still go in and do what needs doing, because you can’t exactly half-show-up as an EMT. But once I’m home, or when I’m supposed to be doing coursework, I get wound up and short, and then I start dropping the things I normally make time for.\n\nLike fishing, woodworking, those first-aid workshops… which sounds funny because the workshops are sort of work-adjacent, but they usually help me feel useful and clear-headed. Since my father-in-law moved in, I just keep skipping them. I tell myself I’m too tired or there’s stuff to deal with at home, and then I end up not going.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're detached from yourself or the world around you?\n\nPatient: Detached from myself… I’m not totally sure what that would feel like. I mean, there are times after a long day where everything feels kind of automatic, like you drive home and realize you don’t remember every turn—but I think everybody does that, right?\n\nMy wife used to joke that I could find my way to the bait shop half-asleep, which, honestly, was probably true. But as far as feeling like I’m outside my body or the world isn’t real… no, I don’t think I’ve really had that.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having any repetitive thoughts or behaviors that you feel compelled to do?\n\nPatient: Repetitive thoughts or behaviors… like checking the stove ten times, or needing things lined up a certain way? No, I don’t think I’ve had anything like that.\n\nI mean, I can get stuck thinking through things sometimes, especially if there’s a lot going on, but I don’t feel like I have to do some ritual or I can’t move on. Although my wife would probably say I have a “system” for the garage, but that’s just because if you don’t keep tools where they belong you spend half the weekend looking for a socket wrench. Since my father-in-law moved in with us, I feel like I’m holding it together fine enough at work, because I have to, but when I get home I’m already wound up and I get short quicker than I want to.\n\nAnd then coursework is the big one I’ll avoid—I’ll sit down to do it and suddenly I’m reorganizing something or telling myself I’ll do it later. Same with the things that usually help me reset, like fishing or woodworking. Even the first-aid workshops, which I used to like going to… I’ve been skipping those too. I tell myself there’s too much going on at home, but I think I’m just trying not to deal with feeling so wound up.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way, with everything feeling off and you being more wound up than usual?\n\nPatient: About four months, I think. It kind of lines up with when my father-in-law moved into our house.\n\nAt first I figured it was just the usual adjustment period, you know—everybody getting used to where the coffee mugs go and who leaves lights on and all that. My house has always had its own rhythm, and then suddenly it didn’t. But yeah… if I’m being honest, that’s when I started feeling more wound up than usual. Four months or so.\n\nDoctor: Are you avoiding certain situations or places because you fear something might happen, or because you're anxious about being in those situations?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it that way. I don’t think I’m avoiding places because I’m afraid something bad is going to happen there.\n\nIt’s more like… if I know a situation is going to be tense or one more thing to handle, I just don’t want to be in it. But not like, “If I go there, something terrible will happen.” It’s not that specific. I mean, I’ve been in plenty of stressful places with my job, and that’s different somehow—you just do what needs doing.\n\nAt home, though, or with certain plans, I’ll kind of steer away from them because I don’t have the bandwidth. But fear of the place itself? No, I haven’t really noticed that.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like things will never get better?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I wouldn’t say I walk around thinking it’ll never get better, exactly. It’s more like when I’m in the middle of it, it feels bigger than it probably is.\n\nLike, I’ll be standing in the kitchen and there’s just noise and people moving around and I start thinking, “How is this supposed to work long-term?” But then, you know, I’ve seen situations change. In my job, you learn that a scene can look completely unmanageable and then five minutes later it’s different because somebody makes one decision or clears one thing out of the way.\n\nSo… I guess I don’t feel hopeless, if that’s what you mean. Just worn down sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Are there any specific activities or situations that you've been avoiding because they make you feel anxious?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I have been avoiding some things, though I don’t think of it as “anxiety” right away.\n\nLike, I used to go fishing, and I’ve got this little woodworking setup—nothing fancy, just enough to make a mess and maybe a crooked shelf if I’m lucky. And I’d sometimes help with first-aid workshops too, which is funny because I do that kind of thing for work, but teaching it is different. It clears my head.\n\nLately I’ve been skipping that stuff. Since my father-in-law moved in, I can hold it together at work pretty well, but when I get home, or when I’m trying to deal with coursework, I’m already wound up and shorter than I mean to be. So if something takes planning or extra energy, even something I normally like, I just… don’t go.\n\nDoctor: Are there any places or social situations that you're avoiding because they make you feel anxious or uncomfortable?\n\nPatient: I guess it’s less a particular place and more the situations that come with it, if that makes sense.\n\nLike, I came in because since my father-in-law moved in with us, I can still keep it together at work—somehow when the radio goes off and there’s a call, you just switch into that mode. But then at home, or when I’m trying to sit down with coursework, I’m already kind of wound tight. I get short faster than I used to.\n\nSo I’ve been skipping things I normally would go do—fishing, messing around with woodworking, even helping with first-aid workshops. Those usually clear my head, but lately they feel like one more thing to manage. Not because I’m scared of the people there exactly… more like I’m uncomfortable and tired before I even get there.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your relationships with family or friends since you started feeling this way?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. Mostly at home. I mean, I came in because ever since my father-in-law moved in with us, I can still hold it together at work—work is work, you know, you’ve got a job in front of you and you do it.\n\nBut at home I’m more wound up. Shorter with people than I’m used to being. And then with coursework, too, it’s like I sit down and I’m already irritated before anything even happens.\n\nWith friends, I guess I’ve pulled back some. I’ve been skipping fishing, not doing much woodworking, and I haven’t been helping with the first-aid workshops like I used to. Those things usually keep my head clear, but lately they just feel like one more thing I have to fit in. So… yeah, I’d say it’s affected things, even if I haven’t really wanted to admit that.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding situations because you're afraid of having some kind of emotional breakdown or losing control?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not like I’m afraid I’m going to fall apart in public or anything.\n\nI mean, losing control is kind of a loaded phrase in my line of work—when you’re on a scene, you’re always thinking about keeping things calm, keeping your voice steady, even when somebody’s yelling or there’s too many people standing around.
